Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. The 2025 licensing round underscores Nigeria’s efforts to strengthen upstream investment and expand exploration activities. According to the NUPRC, 143 companies submitted 200 bids for 37 of the 50 blocks offered during the commercial bid phase. The regulator expects the exercise to support reserve growth and increase production over the next three years. Stakeholders, however, argue that increased investment must be matched by improved protection of oil infrastructure, as pipeline vandalism and crude oil theft remain key challenges to production growth. They also stress the importance of assigning surveillance responsibilities to organisations with experience in protecting oil assets. The Federal Government engaged Tantita Security Services Nigeria Ltd to support the protection of oil assets and enhance security across parts of the Niger Delta. President Bola Tinubu appointed Tantita Security Services Nigeria Limited, led by High Chief Government Ekpemupolo, alias Tompolo, to help safeguard Nigeria’s oil assets in the region. The company works alongside other security agencies in carrying out surveillance activities aimed at protecting oil facilities and supporting stability in the Niger Delta. Stakeholders say improved surveillance has contributed to efforts to reduce pipeline vandalism and crude oil theft while supporting more stable production levels. They also argue that protecting oil infrastructure remains critical to sustaining investment, improving production and supporting government revenue. The Chief Executive Officer of the Centre for the Promotion of Private Enterprise, Muda Yusuf, said Nigeria’s energy transition agenda makes it important to secure existing oil assets while expanding production. He described the 2025 licensing round as an effort to strengthen the country’s energy sector but said improved security must accompany investment initiatives. “We are in the age of energy transition. NUPRC’s operation in this exercise is good for the economy. It will boost foreign reserves, firm up the naira and macroeconomic environment while deepening foreign capital inflows to the domestic economy,” he said. Yusuf added that stronger protection of oil and gas infrastructure could encourage additional investment in the sector. “The new momentum generated will help build capacity to increase the level of production in the oil and gas industry,” he added. Abuja-based energy expert Steven Martins said the outcome of the licensing round represented more than a routine administrative exercise, noting that sustaining oil asset protection would be important as new investments move into the operational phase. “It represents a strategic recalibration of Nigeria’s energy ambitions at a time when global markets are shifting rapidly, and the country faces pressure to strengthen production, attract capital, and reposition itself in the era of energy transition. “As NUPRC is looking forward to harvesting an additional 500 million barrels to the national crude oil reserve from the 2025 bid round, these extra barrels are expected to be driven by the commencement of operational activities of the 31 companies that emerged as winners of 37 oil and gas blocks in the bid round licensing. Protection of oil assets should also form part of the priority areas for stakeholders, and that is where TSSNL should always be supported to achieve its goals,” he said. Martins explained that the awarded assets span several producing and frontier basins, including 16 Niger Delta Onshore blocks, 18 Niger Delta Shallow Water blocks, one Niger Delta Deep Offshore block, three Benin Basin Onshore blocks, four Anambra Basin Onshore blocks, four Chad Basin Onshore blocks and four Benue Trough blocks. The Chief Executive Officer, NUPRC, Mrs Oritsemeyiwa Eyesan, said at the Commercial Bid Conference for the Nigeria Licensing Round, themed “Expanding Opportunities: Right Play, Right Place, Right Time”, that the assets on offer have significant reserve potential. “The assets available in this licensing round have potential to add about 500 million barrels to Nigeria’s reserves, increasing our existing reserves of crude oil and condensate—which currently stand at 37.01 billion barrels—and 215.19 trillion cubic feet of gas,” she said. Eyesan said the absence of bids for the 13 frontier blocks was not unexpected. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. Some winners The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Views from stakeholders Stakeholders have continued to emphasise the importance of pipeline protection to Nigeria’s oil production ambitions, arguing that safeguarding critical infrastructure remains essential to sustaining output, attracting investment and supporting broader economic growth. Related News Morning recap: FG’s N12.62tn debt overshoot, EFCC auction row, Army officer killed, other top headlines Navy foils N660m crude oil theft, arrests 12 suspects in A’Ibom Navy intercepts vessel with N660m stolen crude, arrests 12 suspects In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. The company works alongside other security agencies in carrying out surveillance activities aimed at protecting oil facilities and supporting stability in the Niger Delta. Stakeholders say improved surveillance has contributed to efforts to reduce pipeline vandalism and crude oil theft while supporting more stable production levels. They also argue that protecting oil infrastructure remains critical to sustaining investment, improving production and supporting government revenue. The Chief Executive Officer of the Centre for the Promotion of Private Enterprise, Muda Yusuf, said Nigeria’s energy transition agenda makes it important to secure existing oil assets while expanding production. He described the 2025 licensing round as an effort to strengthen the country’s energy sector but said improved security must accompany investment initiatives. “We are in the age of energy transition. NUPRC’s operation in this exercise is good for the economy. It will boost foreign reserves, firm up the naira and macroeconomic environment while deepening foreign capital inflows to the domestic economy,” he said. Yusuf added that stronger protection of oil and gas infrastructure could encourage additional investment in the sector. “The new momentum generated will help build capacity to increase the level of production in the oil and gas industry,” he added. Abuja-based energy expert Steven Martins said the outcome of the licensing round represented more than a routine administrative exercise, noting that sustaining oil asset protection would be important as new investments move into the operational phase. “It represents a strategic recalibration of Nigeria’s energy ambitions at a time when global markets are shifting rapidly, and the country faces pressure to strengthen production, attract capital, and reposition itself in the era of energy transition. “As NUPRC is looking forward to harvesting an additional 500 million barrels to the national crude oil reserve from the 2025 bid round, these extra barrels are expected to be driven by the commencement of operational activities of the 31 companies that emerged as winners of 37 oil and gas blocks in the bid round licensing. Protection of oil assets should also form part of the priority areas for stakeholders, and that is where TSSNL should always be supported to achieve its goals,” he said. Martins explained that the awarded assets span several producing and frontier basins, including 16 Niger Delta Onshore blocks, 18 Niger Delta Shallow Water blocks, one Niger Delta Deep Offshore block, three Benin Basin Onshore blocks, four Anambra Basin Onshore blocks, four Chad Basin Onshore blocks and four Benue Trough blocks. The Chief Executive Officer, NUPRC, Mrs Oritsemeyiwa Eyesan, said at the Commercial Bid Conference for the Nigeria Licensing Round, themed “Expanding Opportunities: Right Play, Right Place, Right Time”, that the assets on offer have significant reserve potential. “The assets available in this licensing round have potential to add about 500 million barrels to Nigeria’s reserves, increasing our existing reserves of crude oil and condensate—which currently stand at 37.01 billion barrels—and 215.19 trillion cubic feet of gas,” she said. Eyesan said the absence of bids for the 13 frontier blocks was not unexpected. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. Some winners The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Views from stakeholders Stakeholders have continued to emphasise the importance of pipeline protection to Nigeria’s oil production ambitions, arguing that safeguarding critical infrastructure remains essential to sustaining output, attracting investment and supporting broader economic growth. Related News Morning recap: FG’s N12.62tn debt overshoot, EFCC auction row, Army officer killed, other top headlines Navy foils N660m crude oil theft, arrests 12 suspects in A’Ibom Navy intercepts vessel with N660m stolen crude, arrests 12 suspects In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Views from stakeholders Stakeholders have continued to emphasise the importance of pipeline protection to Nigeria’s oil production ambitions, arguing that safeguarding critical infrastructure remains essential to sustaining output, attracting investment and supporting broader economic growth. Related News Morning recap: FG’s N12.62tn debt overshoot, EFCC auction row, Army officer killed, other top headlines Navy foils N660m crude oil theft, arrests 12 suspects in A’Ibom Navy intercepts vessel with N660m stolen crude, arrests 12 suspects In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. “We are in the age of energy transition. NUPRC’s operation in this exercise is good for the economy. It will boost foreign reserves, firm up the naira and macroeconomic environment while deepening foreign capital inflows to the domestic economy,” he said. Yusuf added that stronger protection of oil and gas infrastructure could encourage additional investment in the sector. “The new momentum generated will help build capacity to increase the level of production in the oil and gas industry,” he added. Abuja-based energy expert Steven Martins said the outcome of the licensing round represented more than a routine administrative exercise, noting that sustaining oil asset protection would be important as new investments move into the operational phase. “It represents a strategic recalibration of Nigeria’s energy ambitions at a time when global markets are shifting rapidly, and the country faces pressure to strengthen production, attract capital, and reposition itself in the era of energy transition. “As NUPRC is looking forward to harvesting an additional 500 million barrels to the national crude oil reserve from the 2025 bid round, these extra barrels are expected to be driven by the commencement of operational activities of the 31 companies that emerged as winners of 37 oil and gas blocks in the bid round licensing. Protection of oil assets should also form part of the priority areas for stakeholders, and that is where TSSNL should always be supported to achieve its goals,” he said. Martins explained that the awarded assets span several producing and frontier basins, including 16 Niger Delta Onshore blocks, 18 Niger Delta Shallow Water blocks, one Niger Delta Deep Offshore block, three Benin Basin Onshore blocks, four Anambra Basin Onshore blocks, four Chad Basin Onshore blocks and four Benue Trough blocks. The Chief Executive Officer, NUPRC, Mrs Oritsemeyiwa Eyesan, said at the Commercial Bid Conference for the Nigeria Licensing Round, themed “Expanding Opportunities: Right Play, Right Place, Right Time”, that the assets on offer have significant reserve potential. “The assets available in this licensing round have potential to add about 500 million barrels to Nigeria’s reserves, increasing our existing reserves of crude oil and condensate—which currently stand at 37.01 billion barrels—and 215.19 trillion cubic feet of gas,” she said. Eyesan said the absence of bids for the 13 frontier blocks was not unexpected. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. Some winners The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. “It represents a strategic recalibration of Nigeria’s energy ambitions at a time when global markets are shifting rapidly, and the country faces pressure to strengthen production, attract capital, and reposition itself in the era of energy transition. “As NUPRC is looking forward to harvesting an additional 500 million barrels to the national crude oil reserve from the 2025 bid round, these extra barrels are expected to be driven by the commencement of operational activities of the 31 companies that emerged as winners of 37 oil and gas blocks in the bid round licensing. Protection of oil assets should also form part of the priority areas for stakeholders, and that is where TSSNL should always be supported to achieve its goals,” he said. Martins explained that the awarded assets span several producing and frontier basins, including 16 Niger Delta Onshore blocks, 18 Niger Delta Shallow Water blocks, one Niger Delta Deep Offshore block, three Benin Basin Onshore blocks, four Anambra Basin Onshore blocks, four Chad Basin Onshore blocks and four Benue Trough blocks. The Chief Executive Officer, NUPRC, Mrs Oritsemeyiwa Eyesan, said at the Commercial Bid Conference for the Nigeria Licensing Round, themed “Expanding Opportunities: Right Play, Right Place, Right Time”, that the assets on offer have significant reserve potential. “The assets available in this licensing round have potential to add about 500 million barrels to Nigeria’s reserves, increasing our existing reserves of crude oil and condensate—which currently stand at 37.01 billion barrels—and 215.19 trillion cubic feet of gas,” she said. Eyesan said the absence of bids for the 13 frontier blocks was not unexpected. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. Some winners The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Views from stakeholders Stakeholders have continued to emphasise the importance of pipeline protection to Nigeria’s oil production ambitions, arguing that safeguarding critical infrastructure remains essential to sustaining output, attracting investment and supporting broader economic growth. Related News Morning recap: FG’s N12.62tn debt overshoot, EFCC auction row, Army officer killed, other top headlines Navy foils N660m crude oil theft, arrests 12 suspects in A’Ibom Navy intercepts vessel with N660m stolen crude, arrests 12 suspects In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. “As NUPRC is looking forward to harvesting an additional 500 million barrels to the national crude oil reserve from the 2025 bid round, these extra barrels are expected to be driven by the commencement of operational activities of the 31 companies that emerged as winners of 37 oil and gas blocks in the bid round licensing. Protection of oil assets should also form part of the priority areas for stakeholders, and that is where TSSNL should always be supported to achieve its goals,” he said. Martins explained that the awarded assets span several producing and frontier basins, including 16 Niger Delta Onshore blocks, 18 Niger Delta Shallow Water blocks, one Niger Delta Deep Offshore block, three Benin Basin Onshore blocks, four Anambra Basin Onshore blocks, four Chad Basin Onshore blocks and four Benue Trough blocks. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. Some winners The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Views from stakeholders Stakeholders have continued to emphasise the importance of pipeline protection to Nigeria’s oil production ambitions, arguing that safeguarding critical infrastructure remains essential to sustaining output, attracting investment and supporting broader economic growth. Related News Morning recap: FG’s N12.62tn debt overshoot, EFCC auction row, Army officer killed, other top headlines Navy foils N660m crude oil theft, arrests 12 suspects in A’Ibom Navy intercepts vessel with N660m stolen crude, arrests 12 suspects In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. Eyesan said the absence of bids for the 13 frontier blocks was not unexpected. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. Some winners The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Views from stakeholders Stakeholders have continued to emphasise the importance of pipeline protection to Nigeria’s oil production ambitions, arguing that safeguarding critical infrastructure remains essential to sustaining output, attracting investment and supporting broader economic growth. Related News Morning recap: FG’s N12.62tn debt overshoot, EFCC auction row, Army officer killed, other top headlines Navy foils N660m crude oil theft, arrests 12 suspects in A’Ibom Navy intercepts vessel with N660m stolen crude, arrests 12 suspects In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. Dare Olawin is a journalist at Punch Newspapers with over a decade of reporting experience. He began his career as a community reporter and now covers the energy sector, including oil, gas, electricity, and renewables. Title: South Korea's Naver jumps 10% on Nvidia's $1 billion investment plan
URL: https://finance.yahoo.com/technology/articles/nvidia-acquire-1-billion-shares-230439371.html

Description: Shares of South Korean internet and cloud service giant Naver jumped more than 10% on Monday after it said Nvidia would acquire $1 ‌billion of its new shares...

Title: South Korea's Naver jumps 10% on Nvidia's $1 billion investment plan
URL: https://www.channelnewsasia.com/business/south-koreas-naver-jumps-10-nvidias-1-billion-investment-plan-6279546
Time Published: 2026-07-26T23:04:39Z
Full Content:
East Asia FILE PHOTO: A general view of the Naver sign on its office building in Seongnam, South Korea, May 13, 2022. Picture taken May 13, 2022. REUTERS/ Heo Ran/File Photo SEOUL: Shares of South Korea's internet giant Naver jumped more than 10 per cent on Monday (Jul 27) after it said Nvidia would acquire US$1 billion of its new shares to finance a project to expand an AI data centre. The investment comes after the two firms and investment group Brookfield announced on Friday plans to expand Naver's AI data centre in South Korea with up to US$10 billion in funding. Naver said on Monday it will place 7.2 million new shares with Nvidia for 204,500 won each, or a 1 per cent discount to its closing price on Friday, as part of the agreement. Brookfield will provide up to US$9 billion in financing as the project's capital partner, Naver added. With the investment, Nvidia would become one of the biggest shareholders of Naver, owning a 4.5 per cent stake. The National Pension Service was the biggest shareholder of Naver with a 9.25 per cent stake, followed by BlackRock Fund Advisors with 6.12 per cent as of end-2025. The data centre will be built at Naver's GAK data centre in the city of Sejong in South Korea, using Nvidia's Vera Rubin and Blackwell chip platforms, and it will have a capacity of 200 megawatts by 2028. In June, Naver and Nvidia announced an agreement to jointly pursue the development of gigawatt-scale global AI infrastructure, aiming to tap demand for sovereign AI from the Asia-Pacific region, Europe and the Middle East. One gigawatt is about four times the maximum capacity of GAK Sejong, South Korea's largest hyperscale data centre operated by Naver. It represents a scale capable of accommodating hundreds of thousands of Nvidia's latest GPUs at once, Naver said.
